The test requires each quote in RESULTS.md and, when the card at the pinned revision is in the default cache as `README.md` with sha256 `30f119c55031ca3c70f740ff3dfc395e580fe633bd513be94891a9c3acfdd168`, each quote and the headline sentence in the card with its `**` bold markers removed, and the two `scoreboard.json` quotes on the card's `scoreboard.json` table row, which is the attribution RESULTS.md makes, else it skips. The card is not one of the converter's files (it is fetched by hand), and its bytes differ from the `SHA256SUMS` entry for `README.md`, so the pin is its own
- The evidence README's counts (107 unscored pool cells, 49 dropped questions, 388 rows for 13 models outside the pool, mean success 0.667 and 0.762, 10 domain tasks of 12 to 33) are required in that README as written, and the cached-file test of 12.9 builds the same phrases from the converter report

### 12.11 Judge evidence: the AutoBench committee converter (`evidence/autobench-2026/judges.py`, v1.24; visible answer v1.26)
Purpose: public evidence for the first-class case, choosing a judge model, on 2026 models. AutoBench graded every answer with a committee of judge models and publishes each judge's grade, so a judge outcome matrix in the 12.1 format can be built from it with no API key. Standard library only (argparse, csv, importlib, itertools, json, math, sys, pathlib); the package never imports it
- Reuse: the script loads `convert.py` from its own folder by path with `importlib` and exposes that module as its attribute `convert`. The pinned revision, `FILES`, `fetch`, `verify`, `check_sums`, `pool_models`, `pool_registry`, `TASK_BY`, `read_csv`, `write_jsonl` and its whole-number token check `_count` (v1.25) are that module's (12.9), never copies, so the script holds no sha256 literal and no download code
- Data facts, checked on the pinned files when this section was written: `judges_json` is a JSON object {judge display ID: grade}, with exactly 10 judges on each of the 4,933 scored pool cells and `{}` on the 107 unscored ones; a grade is an int from 1 to 5, a float from 1.1 to 5.0, or `null` (738 nulls); the 14 judges are the pool's `judge_models`, all of them pool members, and `Kimi-k2.6` is listed on only 274 cells and grades 269 of them (5 `null`); the 4,933 cells carry 508 different judge sets, so no judge grades every cell; the dataset's `score` differs by more than 1e-6 from the plain mean of the non-null grades on 4,695 of the 4,933, so `score` only tells scored from unscored cells and the grades are read directly
- Terms: an **answer** is a scored pool cell (one `prompt_id` and the pool model that answered it); a **candidate** is a judge the router chooses among, a model of the output matrix; an answer's **graders** are its judges whose grade is not `null`; for a candidate c on an answer by model a, the **committee** is every grader other than c and a, so an answer model's grade of its own answer never enters a committee
- `judge_matrix(matrix, pool, tolerance=0.5, candidates=None, size=5, min_committee=3, rubric_tokens=400, grade_tokens=200, task_by="all", questions=None, answer_tokens="visible") -> tuple[list[dict], dict]` (`answer_tokens` v1.26, last so that positional calls keep their meaning); `matrix` and `questions` are lists of dicts as `csv.DictReader` yields them, `pool` is the parsed `pool.json`:
  - `ValueError`, before any output, in this order: an invalid pool as in `pool_models`; a `tolerance` that is a bool, not an int or float, not finite or `< 0`; `candidates` that is not `None` and not a non-empty list or tuple, holds an entry that is not a pool display ID, or repeats one; a `size` or `min_committee` (each `>= 1`), or a `rubric_tokens` or `grade_tokens` (each `>= 0`), that is a bool, not an int, or below its bound (`size` is checked even when candidates are given, which then ignore it); an `answer_tokens` that is not one of the strings `visible` and `output` (v1.26); a `task_by` not in `TASK_BY`; with `task_by` `domain`, `questions` that is not a list, or a question with no or a repeated `prompt_id` or no `ddc_domain` as in 12.9 (with `all`, `questions` is never read). Then per matrix row: not a dict, or a `prompt_id` or `model` that is not a non-empty string; with `domain`, a `prompt_id` that names no question; a repeated (`prompt_id`, `model`)
  - a row whose model is not a pool display ID is dropped and counted per name, and a pool row with an empty `score` is counted as unscored, before anything else in it is read. On every other row `judges_json` must be a JSON object with non-empty keys whose values are ints or floats (not bools, finite) or `null`, and `input_tokens` and `output_tokens`, and with `answer_tokens` `visible` also `reasoning_tokens` (v1.26), must be whole numbers `>= 0` as in 12.9 (the `_count` check, so an empty value is an error), checked in that order, so a row with several bad counts names the first; otherwise a `ValueError` naming the cell. With `output`, `reasoning_tokens` is never read, so the rows and the errors are those of v1.25. On the pinned files every scored pool cell has a whole `reasoning_tokens` (0 on 471 of them); the 118 empty values are on 94 of the 107 unscored pool rows and on 24 rows outside the pool, which are never read
  - candidates: the given ones, or by default the `size` judges, among the pool display IDs that grade at least one answer, whose set keeps the most answers under the rules below; ties go to the set whose ascending display IDs come first, which is the first such set in `itertools.combinations` order over the sorted IDs. Judges outside the pool may sit on committees but are never candidates, since they have no price. `ValueError` when `size` is more than those judges. (v1.129) The default search checks the `min_committee` rule below once per answer, groups the answers that pass it by the set of pool judges among their graders other than their answer model, and counts for each candidate set, in `itertools.combinations` order, the answers of the groups whose set holds it. Those are the answers the rules below keep for that set, since a set of pool judges that holds neither the answer model nor a judge outside the graders is exactly a subset of that group set, so the chosen set is the one that applying the rules to every answer for every set gives; the grouping reads each answer once, and the count for each set then grows with the number of distinct groups instead of answers. The rules below then run once per answer, for the chosen set only
  - each answer meets the first of these that applies and is counted under it: its answer model is a candidate (`dropped_self_graded`: generator isolation, 4.2, would never let a model grade its own answer, and dropping the whole answer keeps every candidate on every item); some candidate is not among its graders (`dropped_missing_candidate`, so the matrix is complete as 12.3 requires); it has fewer than `min_committee + 1` graders other than its answer model, so some committee would be smaller than `min_committee` (`dropped_small_committee`); otherwise it is kept. An answer with no graders at all is a missing-candidate drop, and a given candidate that grades no answer is accepted and keeps none, so no committee is ever empty
  - each kept answer is one item: item = `<prompt_id>|<the answer model's mapped ID>`; task = `all`, or the question's `ddc_domain` as written with `task_by` `domain`; one row per candidate with model = its mapped ID, `correct` = `abs(grade - mean) <= tolerance` in floating point as written, with no epsilon, where mean = `math.fsum` of the committee's grades divided by the committee size; `input_tokens` = the cell's `input_tokens` + the answer + `rubric_tokens` (the question, the answer and an assumed rubric), where the answer (v1.26) is `max(output_tokens - reasoning_tokens, 0)` with `answer_tokens` `visible`, the default, and the cell's `output_tokens` with `output`; and `output_tokens` = `grade_tokens` (an assumed grade length). Rows carry no `cost_usd`: they are token-form rows (12.1) that `model-routing evaluate --registry` prices with the registry `pool_registry` builds, at the pool's list prices, so every judge cost is an ESTIMATE, never a billed cost
  - row keys `task`, `item`, `model`, `correct`, `input_tokens`, `output_tokens` in that order, rows sorted by (task, item, model); the result does not depend on the order of the rows of either input
  - report keys, in order: `candidates` (mapped IDs ascending), `candidates_by` (`given` or `most_cells`), `tolerance`, `min_committee`, `rubric_tokens`, `grade_tokens`, `answer_tokens` (v1.26), `task_by`, `scored_cells` (the answers read), `kept` ({task: answers}, tasks ascending), `dropped_self_graded`, `dropped_missing_candidate`, `dropped_small_committee`, `self_graded_cells` (answers whose answer model is among their graders, kept or not: the cells a self-grading check would measure), `unscored_cells`, `dropped_out_of_pool` ({name: rows}, names ascending), `rows`. `scored_cells` equals the kept answers plus the three dropped counts
- The answer a judge reads (v1.26): a judge is shown the answer, never the answer model's reasoning, and a cell's `output_tokens` is the billed output count with the reasoning in it. On the 4,058 billed rows of 12.9, the `--registry` list prices times `input_tokens` and `output_tokens` give a median ratio of 1.000 to the billed `cost`, and 1.710 when `reasoning_tokens` is added on top (checked when this was written; 13.9 finds 1.001 and 1.718 on the 4,931 billed scored cells). So `visible`, the output less the reasoning, is the default estimate of the answer's length. The two counts are not exact: on 104 of the 4,933 scored cells `reasoning_tokens` is above `output_tokens`, by at most 21%, and there the floor counts the answer as 0 tokens, an underestimate, since its true length is unknown. `output` keeps the v1.25 estimate, an upper bound that counts the reasoning as read. The option changes only `input_tokens`: the candidates, the drops, the items and every `correct` are the same under both
- What the label is: a leave-one-out committee label, agreement with the other judges, and not a human gold label. A judge that always grades as the committee does scores 1.0 whether or not the committee is right, and a judge that is right where the committee is wrong scores lower. Answers to the same question are separate items, so the 12.2 split can put answers to one question in both halves
- CLI: `python evidence/autobench-2026/judges.py --out PATH [--cache DIR] [--registry PATH] [--tolerance T] [--candidates A,B,...] [--size K] [--min-committee N] [--rubric-tokens N] [--grade-tokens N] [--task-by {domain,all}] [--answer-tokens {visible,output}]`, each option defaulting to the function's (`--task-by` to `all`, `--answer-tokens`, v1.26, to `visible`; any other value is a usage error). Options are checked first, so a bad one downloads nothing: `--out`, and `--registry` when it is given, must pass the 12.14 `--out` check, run by `convert._check_out`, never a copy, in that order and before the four-files check and the `--candidates` check below (v1.174, row H4b; to v1.173 only those two came before `convert.fetch`, so a bad `--out` or `--registry` failed at the write or the rename after the download). It calls `convert.fetch(--cache)`, runs `judge_matrix` on the cached `matrix.csv` and `pool.json` (and `questions.csv` with `domain`) with `--candidates` split on commas, writes the rows as JSONL and, with `--registry`, `json.dumps(pool_registry(pool), indent=2)` and a newline (byte-identical to `convert.py --registry`), each through `<path>.part`, both written before either is renamed, with the `.part` files removed on failure; `--out`, `--registry` and their two `.part` paths must be four different files, as in 12.9. It prints `json.dumps(report, indent=2)` once the `.part` files are written and before either rename, flushing stdout (v1.94, as 13.11), so a write to stdout that fails (a closed pipe) is an `OSError` that leaves `--out` and `--registry` as they were (after a `BrokenPipeError` from the print, stdout is pointed at `os.devnull` as in 13.11, v1.150, so the process exits 2 with one `error:` line, not 120 with two more lines from the interpreter's flush at exit), and exits 0, or exits 2 with one `error: ...` line on stderr (line breaks escaped) for a usage error (including an empty name in `--candidates`) or any `ValueError`, `OSError` or `csv.Error`; a rename that fails after the print is the one failure with the report already on stdout. A failure before the renames writes no output file and leaves an existing one as it was; the two renames are not atomic together, so a failure in the second leaves `--out` renamed and `--registry` not, as in 12.9
- On the pinned files (a private prototype of this contract, run when this section was written, and the script since v1.25; the cached-file tests pin these): the default candidates are `deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash-0731`, `google/gemma-4-31b-it`, `meta/muse-glimmer-30b`, `openai/gpt-5.6-luna` and `tencent/hy3`; 756 of the 4,933 answers are kept (1,252 self-graded, 2,925 missing a candidate, 0 with a small committee), 3,780 rows; 2,478 answers are self-graded in all (2,514 list their answer model among their judges when `null` grades count); 107 unscored cells and 388 out-of-pool rows over 13 names. `size` 5 is the default as a trade between candidates and items: each candidate more cuts the kept answers by about a third (1,191 at 4, 756 at 5, 460 at 6), and at 5 the router still has 756 answer items, against the 203 question items of the 12.9 answer matrix. On the 756 kept answers, 707 report reasoning tokens, their `reasoning_tokens` summing to 0.738 of their `output_tokens`, and on 14 `reasoning_tokens` is above `output_tokens`, by at most 10% (counts of v1.25). v1.26: the floor applies to those 14, and with the default `visible` the judge input is 1,218,584 tokens per candidate over the 756 kept answers, 0.406 of the 3,001,074 that `output` counts (v1.26)
- Test status: `tests/test_evidence_autobench_judges.py` was committed before the script, under a module-level `pytest.mark.xfail(strict=True, raises=ModuleNotFoundError)`, which the implementing commit removed (v1.25). All but five tests use tiny in-memory tables. Two read the evidence README (`evidence/autobench-2026/README.md`, section `Judge evidence: one item per graded answer`), which states the regenerate line, every assumption above with its option and default, and the counts on the pinned files. Three read the pinned files only when they are already cached and skip otherwise: the default report, the rows feeding `evaluate`, and every count of that README section built from the report and `matrix.csv` and required as written, as the 12.10 counts test does. v1.26: the `answer_tokens` tests (the subtraction and its floor, the option check, `reasoning_tokens` read only with `visible`, the report key, the CLI option) were committed failing before the code, and the counts test also builds the two judge input totals from the rows of both options

### 12.12 Judge results on AutoBench (`evidence/autobench-judges/results.json`, v1.27; routing and whole-prose checks v1.29)
Purpose: the evaluator's numbers for choosing a judge model on public data, in the shape of 12.7 and 12.10, regenerated byte for byte from stated commands, with the table and every number RESULTS.md states checked against the committed file or, for the sensitivity runs, against the pinned files when they are cached
- Script: `evidence/routerbench/results.py` (12.7) is reused unchanged, as in 12.10. The judge rows carry token counts and no cost, so `evaluate` prices them from the registry `judges.py --registry` writes (12.1 token form, 12.11), and every `cost_usd` in the file is a list-price estimate, never a billed cost
- Committed: `evidence/autobench-judges/results.json`, from the 12.11 judge matrix at its default options (one task, `all`, of 756 answer items over the 5 default candidates) and `model-routing evaluate --registry <that registry> --bar 0.6 0.7 0.8 0.9 --seeds 50 --json` at the default `min_samples` 30 and confidence 0.95, reduced by `results.py --evaluate`; and `evidence/autobench-judges/RESULTS.md` with the five-line regenerate block, the `table` output in a fenced `text` block whose first line is `$ python evidence/routerbench/results.py --table evidence/autobench-judges/results.json`, the size of the committed file in bytes, and the sections below
- Names: the best single judge is the best model of 12.7 (highest `success` of the first (bar, flavor), ties by lower cost, then by name); the cheapest candidate is the `model:` row of the first (bar, flavor) with the lowest `cost_usd`, ties by name. A cost ratio is a routed `cost_usd` over the best single judge's, to 3 decimals; success to 3 decimals and cost to 4, as in 12.7
- Intro: the number of answer items, of candidates and of seeds, and the 12.11 default tolerance, each from the committed file or the script's default; a header paragraph names the best single judge as the `vs_best_model` divisor. v1.29: the test builds the whole intro paragraph, the whole header paragraph (the one before the table block) and the whole Caveats list from the committed file, the 12.11 defaults and the converter's dataset and revision, and requires each to equal its RESULTS.md text, so a number added beside a checked phrase fails, as it does for the table and the Reading it bullets
- Reading it, each bullet built from the committed file by the test and required verbatim:
  - every seed clears: the bars at which `cost_effective` clears on every seed and routes all 50 seeds to one judge, which must be the cheapest candidate, with its held-out success, its cost ratio, and the best single judge's success and cost
  - partly and never: the first bar at which `cost_effective` clears on some seeds but not all, with the cleared seed count, the seeds per routed judge (most seeds first, then by name), its success and cost ratio; then the first bar at which no seed clears, with the best single judge's held-out success; the fallback judge is the one with the highest calibration-half Wilson lower bound by the 5.3 `best_available` sort, which the per-seed check below confirms on the pinned files (v1.29)
  - `cost_blind`: its success, cost ratio and seeds per routed judge, which must be the same at every bar, and whether it is level with `best_on_calibration` at full precision at every bar (said as "level with" only then); it routes to the judge with the highest calibration-half Wilson lower bound because under `cost_blind` both 5.3 sorts reduce to (minus the lower bound, ID) when every candidate has at least `min_samples` calibration answers, which the per-seed check below confirms on the pinned files (v1.29)
  - bar violations: each routed rate above 0 as a count of the seeds that cleared (one task, so a rate counts seeds), next to the nominal one-sided rate `(1 - confidence) / 2` of the Wilson bound
  - the oracle: its success and cost; while its success is below 1, that where no candidate agrees with the committee it takes the cheapest candidate on that answer, counted wrong (12.3), with that share of the held-out answers, 1 minus its success, and when it is 1, that some candidate agrees on every held-out answer; next to the best single judge's success
  - price: the two candidates with the highest cost, named with their success and cost, stated to be the two that agree least only when they are; `openai/gpt-5.6-luna`, when it is one of them, is named as the answer model of the dataset card's headline (12.10)
- Sensitivity, stated in RESULTS.md with the commands that give it and not committed as files: `--tolerance 1.0`, and `--answer-tokens output` (12.11, the judge reads the reasoning too, an upper bound on cost with the same labels), each at bar 0.8 with 50 seeds; for each, the best single judge's line and both routed lines in the line format of 12.10. The `--tolerance 1.0` matrix has the default matrix's (task, item, model) rows in the same order, every default success stays a success and more rows are successes, which RESULTS.md states as more of the judges' grades counting as successes; the `--answer-tokens output` matrix has the same rows with the same labels and no row with fewer input tokens, and RESULTS.md says only of its three lines, which the test checks against the committed file, that held-out success, cleared and bar-violation rates are those of the default run and only the costs rise
- Caveats: the committee label is agreement with the other judges, not a human gold label; the cost is a list-price estimate at the pool's prices with the assumed rubric and grade tokens of 12.11; the candidates are the 5 that keep the most answers, not chosen by quality or price; answers to one question are separate items and the split can put them in both halves; the licence, dataset URL and pinned revision; a link to the 12.11 evidence README section
- Tests, in `tests/test_evidence_autobench_judges_results.py`: the committed file is under 300,000 bytes, is exactly `json.dumps(reduce(itself), indent=2)` plus a newline, has that config, the one task `all` with 756 items and the 5 default candidates of 12.11, unique summary and per-task keys and 50 seeds behind every `routed_models`; RESULTS.md holds the table block, the two command blocks, the intro, the header, the file size and every Reading it bullet above, built from the file, and every other number of its prose (seed counts, the sensitivity bar, the candidate count, section references) as written; two tests read the pinned files only when cached and skip otherwise (CI never downloads them): one rebuilds the committed bytes in-process from `judge_matrix` and `evaluate`, the other rebuilds every sensitivity line and checks the matrix comparisons and the higher costs of the sensitivity bullet. The tests were committed before the files under a module-level `pytest.mark.xfail(strict=True, raises=FileNotFoundError)`, which the commit that adds the files removes. v1.29: when the pinned files are cached (12.9), a test rebuilds the matrix, runs `evaluate` with its cells at the committed config, and for every (bar, flavor, seed) cell recomputes each candidate's calibration-half successes over the 12.2 split (its hash rule re-derived in the test), its `wilson_lower_bound` at the run's confidence and its mean calibration cost, and requires the cell's `cleared` and routed judge to be what 5.3 gives: when some candidate's lower bound reaches the bar, the first such candidate by (routing cost, minus the lower bound, ID), else the first candidate by (minus the lower bound, routing cost, ID), with routing cost 0 under `cost_blind`; every calibration half must hold at least `min_samples` answers, and the routed seed counts per (bar, flavor) must equal the committed `routed_models`. The routed judge must differ from the cheapest by mean calibration cost in some fallback cell and in some `cost_blind` cell. Every candidate is graded on the same calibration half, so its Wilson lower bound orders the candidates as their calibration success does, and the check confirms the routed judge is the highest on both. CI never downloads the files, so there it skips

### 12.13 Regeneration on GitHub (`.github/workflows/evidence.yml`, v1.30; pinned runner v1.49; LiveCodeBench v1.65; delegation on tau2-bench v1.80; its pooled run v1.84; token permissions v1.137; time limit v1.152, restated v1.170, v1.217, v1.225, v1.268, v1.301, v1.302 and v1.321; LLMRouterBench v1.168; delegation on LLMRouterBench v1.200; one run of a repeated line v1.215; no cache between runs v1.251; LLM Routing Response Bank v1.298; delegation on the LLM Routing Response Bank v1.316)
Purpose: a reader can see the AutoBench-based results files, from v1.55 the tau2-bench one, from v1.65 the LiveCodeBench one, from v1.80 the tau2-bench delegation one, from v1.84 the pooled tau2-bench delegation one, from v1.168 the LLMRouterBench one, from v1.200 the LLMRouterBench delegation one and from v1.299 the response-bank one regenerate byte for byte from the public data. The cached-file tests of 12.10, 12.12, 12.15, 12.17, 12.19, 12.21, 13.10, 13.12 and 13.13 show it only where the pinned files are cached, and `ci.yml` never downloads them, so on GitHub those tests skip
- The workflow `.github/workflows/evidence.yml`, `name: evidence`, runs on `workflow_dispatch` and on `push` to `main` limited by `paths` to `evidence/**`, `model_routing/**`, `pyproject.toml` and the workflow file itself; `permissions: contents: read`; `bash` as the default `run` shell (so a failing command stops the step); no secret, no `pull_request_target`, no write permission. `README.md` is not in `paths`: a push that changes a command in one of the README regenerate blocks the workflow reruns (three to v1.54, four from v1.55, five from v1.65, six from v1.80, from v1.84 seven: one per rerun subsection and the pooled one of `### Delegation on tau2-bench`, from v1.168 eight, with the one of `### LLMRouterBench`, from v1.200 nine, with the one of `### Delegation on LLMRouterBench`, and from v1.299 ten, with the one of `### LLM Routing Response Bank`) fails `tests/test_evidence_workflow.py` in `ci.yml` unless the workflow changes with it, and a change to the workflow runs it
- One job, `regenerate`, holding only `runs-on` (that of `ci.yml`), from v1.152 `timeout-minutes` (the time-limit bullet below), and `steps`, with the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-python` versions of `ci.yml`; checkout keeps no credentials (`persist-credentials: false`), and setup-python installs one Python version, one of those in the `ci.yml` matrix
- Runner (v1.49): `ci.yml` and this workflow each have one `runs-on`, the versioned image label `ubuntu-24.04`, the same in both, never `ubuntu-latest`. GitHub moves `ubuntu-latest` to Ubuntu 26.04 between 2026-10-19 and 2026-11-19 (runner-images issue 14748, the notice on each of the 5 check runs of v1.48, all of its second commit, since its two commits were pushed together), and a pinned label keeps each run on the image the suite last passed on, so a move of the label does not change the image under a push (a new release of an action or of a Python build still can change a run with no code change). In each file, the column-0 comment lines directly above `jobs:` say the runner is pinned to that image, with the date of the pin and the reason. Moving the label is a change to both files together, kept only after a green run on the new label; on 2026-11-06 `actions/python-versions` (revision 0bd7e714) lists x64 builds for Ubuntu 26.04 of every `ci.yml` matrix version (3.10.22, 3.11.18, 3.12.16 and 3.13.18), so setup-python could serve the matrix there
- Time limit and retries (v1.152, value restated v1.170, v1.217, v1.225, v1.268, v1.301, v1.302 and v1.321): the job `regenerate` sets `timeout-minutes: 35`, the header line directly after its `runs-on`, so a run whose download stalls without failing ends after 35 minutes instead of holding the job to GitHub's default limit of 360 minutes (row CI3, review iter-077 nit 6). Basis (v1.217, row E17h; restated v1.225, row E17i, v1.268, row CI10, v1.301, row CI11, v1.302, row CI12, and v1.321, row CI14): from v1.217 the slowest run on the `Regenerate` lines in force, that is a run of a commit whose `Regenerate` step holds the lines it holds now (from v1.215 those the test rebuilds from the README by the repeated-line rule below), since a run of other lines times another job (fewer lines understate it, a line run twice overstates it). Every run on those lines counts, before or after a change elsewhere, so a converter, script or pinned file that makes the same lines slower shows as a slower run of them and restates the value by the rule; a change of the lines starts the basis again from the first run on the new ones, and until that run is measured the value in force stands (v1.218, review iter-157 should-fix 1). From v1.299 the lines in force are the v1.215 lines with the four `### LLM Routing Response Bank` lines after the `### LLMRouterBench` ones (the response-bank bullet below), so no run of the v1.215 lines alone is the basis any more (v1.301, row CI11). From v1.317 the lines in force are the v1.299 lines with the three `### Delegation on the LLM Routing Response Bank` lines it runs after the three `### Delegation on LLMRouterBench` ones (the response-bank delegation bullet below), so no run of the v1.299 lines alone is the basis any more (v1.321, row CI14). The 79 runs of this workflow that `gh run list --limit 100` returned at v1.321 (2026-11-13), 42120048976 (2026-11-05) to 43439800863 (2026-11-13), all succeeded, and the last two run the v1.317 lines, which `8642260` added and which reached GitHub with `e393219` in one push: 43433217023 on `e393219` (204 seconds from its start to its last update), the first, and 43439800863 on `17ef05b` (206), whose `Regenerate` step holds the lines of `e393219`'s, since `17ef05b` changed no line of the workflow. The slowest is run 43439800863 on `17ef05b`, which took 206 seconds from its start to its last update (its `regenerate` job 203 seconds by its own start and end times and its `Regenerate` step 196); the limit is ten times that, rounded up to a whole five minutes: 2,060 seconds is 34.3 minutes, so 35. The first run alone gives the same value (2,040 seconds is 34.0 minutes). Where the time went (v1.321, row CI14): by the two runs' job log timestamps, each gap read as the v1.301 basis below reads it, the 13.14 script line, from the last line the LLMRouterBench `--bar 0.1 --point 0` table printed to the first line of the response-bank delegation table, took 9.3 seconds in run 43433217023 (13:26:34.76Z to 13:26:44.04Z) and 10.5 in run 43439800863 (14:11:00.40Z to 14:11:10.93Z), against the 4.5 seconds 13.14's prototype ran the script in (the response-bank delegation bullet below), and the three new lines, to the last line of the response-bank `--bar 0.8 --point 0` table, 9.4 and 10.6; the LLMRouterBench converter line took 39.0 and 47.3 seconds, the response-bank converter line 9.1 and 27.0, its evaluate, results and `--table` lines 3.5 and 4.4, and the 13.13 script on the LLMRouterBench matrix 28.3 and 31.6. So the two converter lines, which download their pinned files, took 48.1 and 74.3 seconds together, against 67.2 and 176.2 in the two runs on the v1.299 lines, and both runs were shorter than those two (213 and 281 seconds) although they ran three more lines. The value is the second restatement that lowers it (v1.301 the first), as the v1.218 restart allows once the first run on new lines is measured; 2,100 seconds is still 7.5 times the 281 seconds of the slowest run on the v1.299 lines, which these lines hold. A slow download counts as any other run does: the limit covers the slowest run measured on these lines, and the workflow keeps no cache between runs (the no-cache bullet below, v1.251). By the rule a slowest run of 181 to 210 seconds gives 35, 211 to 240 gives 40, 241 to 270 gives 45, 271 to 300 gives 50, 301 to 330 gives 55 and 331 to 360 gives 60, so a run on these lines over 210 seconds restates the value (v1.225; 330 from v1.268, 240 from v1.301, 300 from v1.302, 210 from v1.321). The v1.302 basis was the slower of the two runs then on the v1.299 lines among the 74 runs of this workflow that `gh run list --limit 100` returned at v1.302 (2026-11-12), 42120048976 (2026-11-05) to 43363202337 (2026-11-12), all successful: 43357001972 on `fb864c7` (213 seconds from its start to its last update) and 43363202337 on `303b4cb` (281), whose `Regenerate` step held the lines of `fb864c7`'s, since `303b4cb` changed only the workflow's limit line and top-comment sentence; it was run 43363202337 on `303b4cb`, which took 281 seconds from its start to its last update (its `regenerate` job 279 seconds by its own start and end times and its `Regenerate` step 273), and gave 50 (2,810 seconds is 46.8 minutes). Where the time went (v1.302, row CI12): by the run's job log timestamps, each gap read as the v1.301 basis below reads it, the LLMRouterBench converter line took 118.6 seconds (04:06:38.19Z to 04:08:36.83Z), against 42.1 in run 43357001972; the response-bank converter line 57.6 (04:08:42.85Z to 04:09:40.44Z), against 25.1; its evaluate, results and `--table` lines 2.1 (04:09:40.44Z to 04:09:42.56Z), against 4.0; and the 13.13 script on the LLMRouterBench matrix 16.0 (04:09:50.99Z to 04:10:06.99Z), against 32.0. So the two converter lines, which download their pinned files, took 76.5 and 32.5 seconds more than in the run before, 109.0 together, more than the 68 seconds by which this run was longer, while the lines that read only files already on the runner took less; the extra time most likely went to the downloads, and the four response-bank lines took about 59.7 seconds of the run. The v1.301 basis was the one run then on the v1.299 lines among the 73 runs of this workflow that `gh run list --limit 100` returned at v1.301 (2026-11-12), 42120048976 (2026-11-05) to 43357001972 (2026-11-12), all successful: run 43357001972 on `fb864c7`, which took 213 seconds from its start to its last update (its `regenerate` job 210 seconds by its own start and end times and its `Regenerate` step 200), and gave 40 (2,130 seconds is 35.5 minutes), the first restatement that lowered the value (from 55), as the v1.218 restart allows once the first run on new lines is measured; 2,400 seconds was still 7.7 times the 312 seconds of the slowest run on the v1.215 lines, which these lines hold, and 6.8 times the about 351 seconds the response-bank bullet expected. Where the time went (v1.301, row CI11): by the run's job log timestamps the LLMRouterBench converter line, from the last line the LiveCodeBench table printed to the first line the converter printed, took 42.1 seconds (03:10:28.98Z to 03:11:11.06Z), against 42.9 to 178.7 in the five runs the v1.225 and v1.268 bases read; the response-bank converter line, from the last line the LLMRouterBench table printed to the first line the converter printed, 25.1 (03:11:20.07Z to 03:11:45.12Z), its download of the four pinned files, their size and sha256 checks and its read together, since the log holds no line between the two; its evaluate, results and `--table` lines, from the last line of the converter's report to the first line of the table, 4.0 (03:11:45.12Z to 03:11:49.16Z); and the 13.13 script on the LLMRouterBench matrix, from the last line the pooled tau2-bench table printed to the first line of its own table, 32.0 (03:12:04.67Z to 03:12:36.68Z), against 29.7 to 61.6. So the four new lines took about 29.1 seconds of the run. The v1.268 basis was the slowest of the 11 runs on the v1.215 lines among the 64 runs of this workflow that `gh run list --limit 100` returned at v1.268 (2026-11-11), 42120048976 (2026-11-05) to 43074504807 (2026-11-11), all successful: the four of the v1.225 basis, 42883099079 on `d91a24c` (213 seconds from its start to its last update), 42893385889 on `715d408` (219), 42904515973 on `591bd85` (290) and 42913311586 on `fa907b9` (275), and the seven since, 42930309837 on `12ebd50` (207), 42964762032 on `781c3e7` (197), 42978312279 on `2edd7fa` (121), 43008722362 on `bca824c` (157), 43054947572 on `f7bf4bc` (312), 43065316899 on `cdb8569` (209) and 43074504807 on `5f54772` (284); it was run 43054947572 on `f7bf4bc`, which took 312 seconds from its start to its last update (its `regenerate` job 290 seconds by its own start and end times and its `Regenerate` step 281), and gave 55 (3,120 seconds is 52.0 minutes). Where the time went (v1.225, row E17i): by the job logs' timestamps the LLMRouterBench converter line, from the last line the LiveCodeBench table printed to the first line the converter printed, took 55.0, 42.9, 129.4 and 131.2 seconds of the four runs of the v1.225 basis in that order, and the 13.13 script on the LLMRouterBench matrix, from the last line the pooled tau2-bench table printed to the first line of its own table, took 56.5, 61.6, 55.4 and 48.4, so the two slow runs spent their extra time in the converter line, not in the delegation code the commits of rows P1b and P1c changed. That line prints nothing before its JSON report, so its gap is the download of the pinned 1,283,503,080-byte tarball, its size and sha256 check and its read together, which the logs do not separate; the check and read of the cached file alone took 29.9 to 39.4 seconds of the runs to v1.214 (their second converter run, below), so the 74 to 88 seconds the slow runs add most likely went to the download (v1.226, review iter-161 should-fix 1). In run 43054947572 (v1.268, row CI10) the converter line took 178.7 seconds by the same timestamps (16:07:28.90Z to 16:10:27.64Z), against 42.9 to 131.2 in the four runs of the v1.225 basis, and the 13.13 script on the LLMRouterBench matrix 29.7 (16:10:52.86Z to 16:11:22.54Z), against 48.4 to 61.6, so its extra time again went to the converter line, and, less the 29.9 to 39.4 seconds of a check and read of the cached file, about 139 to 149 seconds of it most likely to the download. The four runs on v1.200 to v1.214 SHAs, 42814244636 (271 seconds), 42832147932 (237), 42842948840 (264) and 42874000096 (278, on `34310a8`, the slowest of the 54 runs of v1.217), are not the basis: each ran the LLMRouterBench converter line a second time, the line v1.215 leaves out, and that second run took 39.4, 29.9, 35.4 and 37.9 seconds of them by their logs (from the last line the pooled tau2-bench table printed to the first line the second converter run printed). Less their second converter run, the four would have taken 231.6, 207.1, 228.6 and 240.1 seconds, the last just past the 240 seconds up to which the rule gives 40; these are not the basis either, since subtracting a gap read from a log estimates a run of other lines and does not measure one (v1.218, review iter-157 should-fix 2). The v1.225 basis was the slowest of the four runs then on the v1.215 lines, 42904515973 on `591bd85`, 290 seconds, which gave 50 (2,900 seconds is 48.3 minutes). The v1.217 basis was the one run then on the v1.215 lines, 42883099079 on `d91a24c`, 213 seconds (its `regenerate` job 210 seconds by its own start and end times and its `Regenerate` step 200), which gave 40 (2,130 seconds is 35.5 minutes). The v1.170 basis was the 45 runs to 42717985363, the slowest 158 seconds (run 42717985363 on `ac17c1f`, the first with the LLMRouterBench lines), which gave 30; the v1.152 basis was the 40 runs to 42622487042, the slowest 140 seconds (run 42445989566 on `227bbea`), which gave 25. The converters' socket timeout does not bound a run: the `fetch` functions the job calls, three to v1.167 (12.9, 12.14 and 12.16; `judges.py` calls the one of 12.9), from v1.168 four (with that of 12.18) and from v1.299 five (with that of 12.20), each open a file with one `urlopen(..., timeout=60)`, a limit on each blocking socket operation, not on the download, so a server that sends a few bytes inside every 60 seconds holds the call for as long as it keeps sending. A change of the value states its new basis in this bullet in the same commit. Retries: none in the converters, by choice. A download that fails or times out makes `fetch` raise, its converter exit with a non-zero status, and `Regenerate` stop under the `bash` default shell; on GitHub the retry is a rerun of the job from the Actions tab (or a `workflow_dispatch` run), which downloads every file again, since the workflow keeps no cache between runs; locally it is a rerun of the same command, which downloads only the files the failed run did not finish, since `fetch` keeps each verified file in the cache and checks a cached file's size and sha256 instead of downloading it again. A bounded retry inside `fetch` would add a path to three converters (four from v1.168, five from v1.299), each needing its own test, for a failure none of the 79 runs above had; a run that fails on a download is the point to read its log and weigh a retry again. The column-0 comment above `jobs:` ends, after the runner sentences, with the sentence `The job's time limit of 35 minutes is about ten times its slowest run, and a failed download is retried by rerunning the job (DESIGN.md 12.13).` Tests in `tests/test_evidence_workflow.py` require the job header to be `jobs:`, `  regenerate:`, the `runs-on` line and `    timeout-minutes: 35`, in that order, with exactly one header line that names `timeout`, the comment above `jobs:` to end with that sentence, and the limit to be ten times the recorded 206 seconds rounded up to whole five minutes and under 360, and (v1.217) this bullet to give that value and, in its basis before the first history sentence (v1.225, from v1.268 the v1.225 one, from v1.301 the v1.268 one, from v1.302 the v1.301 one, from v1.321 the v1.302 one), the run 43439800863 and its 206 seconds in one clause (v1.218), the arithmetic to 35 and (v1.225) the run length past which the value is restated, six times the value in seconds (210); and each of 5 broken workflows fails the time-limit check it names: the limit dropped, raised by a minute (to 36), set to 360, or moved from the job to the `Regenerate` step (the limit line), and the sentence dropped from the comment (the comment)
- No cache between runs (v1.251, row CI9): the workflow keeps no cache, by choice, so each run downloads the pinned files of the four sources whose converters it runs, the 5 AutoBench files (2,207,795 bytes, which the AutoBench converter fetches and `judges.py` gets through the same 12.9 `fetch`), the 12 tau2-bench run files (295,933,742), the 67 LiveCodeBench files (1,233,548,121) and the LLMRouterBench tarball (1,283,503,080), 2,815,192,738 bytes in all, and from v1.299 the 4 response-bank files (1,018,191,580, 12.20) as a fifth source, 3,833,384,318 bytes in all, and checks each one's size and sha256 before it reads it. The reason is the purpose of this section: a run that downloads also shows that each source still serves the pinned bytes at its pinned URL, so the regenerate commands still work for a reader, and a cache hit would skip that check for as long as its entry lives. GitHub's dependency caching reference (https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/dependency-caching, read 2026-11-11) removes an entry only when it has not been accessed in over 7 days, and the 61 runs of this workflow that `gh run list --limit 100` returned on 2026-11-11, from 2026-11-05 to 2026-11-11, all succeeded and none started more than 9.2 hours after the one before, so an entry keyed on a pinned sha256, which never changes, would stay alive for as long as the workflow runs at least once in every 7 days, as it has since 2026-11-05, and a source that moved or changed its bytes would go unseen until a run missed the cache. Size is not the reason: the same reference gives each repository 10 GB of cache by default, which the 2.82 GB would fit (3.83 GB from v1.299). Nor is the integrity of a hit: `fetch` checks a cached file's size and sha256 before it reads it, on GitHub as locally (12.9). Time does not call for a cache either: the part of a run that varies is the LLMRouterBench download, to which the time-limit bullet gives, most likely, the 74 to 88 seconds the two slow runs of its v1.225 basis added, and the slowest run on these lines took 290 seconds against a limit of 50 minutes (from v1.268, 312 against 55; from v1.301, the one run on the v1.299 lines, 213 against 40; from v1.302, the slower of the two, 281 against 50; from v1.321, the slower of the two runs on the v1.317 lines, 206 against 35), a rule that already counts a slow download as a run. In run 43008722362 on `bca824c` (2026-11-11), 157 seconds from its start to its last update and its `Regenerate` step 145, that converter line took 42.5 seconds by the log's timestamps (from the last line the LiveCodeBench table printed to the first line the converter printed), just under the 42.9 to 131.2 seconds of the four basis runs. A hit would save at most that download less the time of a restore, which still moves the 1,283,503,080 bytes, from GitHub's cache instead of the source. The point to weigh a cache again is a run that fails on a download or nears its limit. The step checks of the tests bullet below already hold the decision, since a cache is a step of its own or an input of setup-python, and each of two broken workflows (v1.251) fails the one check it names: an `actions/cache` step keyed on the LLMRouterBench converter's pinned `SHA256`, before `Install` (the step-name check), and `cache: pip` under setup-python (the setup-python check)
- Token permissions (v1.137): `ci.yml`, like this workflow, sets one top-level `permissions` block, the line `permissions:` and the one line `  contents: read` under it, and no job of either file sets its own `permissions`, so the `GITHUB_TOKEN` of every job of both workflows has `contents: read` and, since a block that names one scope sets each scope it does not name to `none` (GitHub's workflow syntax reference), `none` for every other scope of that reference's `permissions` table, by the file. Neither workflow needs more: `ci.yml` checks the code out, installs it and runs the suite, the demo, the pandas step and the wheel step, and pushes, comments, labels and uploads nothing. Without the block a workflow's token takes the repository's default, which `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/actions/permissions/workflow` gave for this repository as `"default_workflow_permissions":"read"` on 2026-11-08, so until v1.138 the read-only token of `ci.yml` held by a setting outside the repository, which a change of that setting or a fork whose own default is `write` would widen. Tests in `tests/test_ci_permissions.py` (standard library only, reading both files with the `top_blocks` reader of `tests/test_evidence_workflow.py`) require each file to have exactly one top-level `permissions` block, that text exactly, and no line that names `permission` in any letter case, by substring, but one that starts with `permissions:` at column 0 (read by the block check), so a job's own block, a comment naming permissions or a column-0 `permissions : ...` (v1.139) fails, as the `secrets` check of this workflow fails closed; and each of 10 broken pairs (11 from v1.139), checked after the pair as committed passes, fails with the one problem it names: the block dropped from either file, `contents: write` in either file, a second scope (`pull-requests: write`) in `ci.yml`, `permissions: write-all` in `ci.yml`, `permissions: read-all` in this workflow, a job of either file with its own `permissions`, a second top-level block at the end of `ci.yml`, and (v1.139) a second key `permissions : write-all` at column 0 in `ci.yml`, which YAML reads as `permissions` and the block reader as another key
- Python versions (v1.118): the `ci.yml` matrix is `["3.10", "3.11", "3.12", "3.13", "3.14"]`, the one `python-version` list of the job `test`, every minor version from the lowest to the highest in ascending order, and (v1.120, review) the only key of its `strategy.matrix`, with no `include` or `exclude`, while every other `ci.yml` line that names `python-version` only reads `matrix.python-version`, so the list is the Python of every job; `pyproject.toml` keeps `requires-python = ">=3.10"`, the lowest matrix version, so every Python minor version from the floor the package states through the newest one tested has a CI job; and the line in the README `## Development` section says `CI runs the suite and the demo on Python 3.10 to 3.14`, the first and last matrix versions. The basis is the Python devguide status page (https://devguide.python.org/versions/, read 2026-11-08): 3.15 (first release 2026-10-09) and 3.14 (first release 2025-10-07) in bugfix, 3.13, 3.12 and 3.11 in security, and 3.10 end-of-life since 2026-10-01; v1.118 specifies adding 3.14, which the v1.117 matrix lacked, and v1.119 adds it. 3.10 stays: the package is standard library only and passes on it, `actions/setup-python` still installs it on the pinned runner (the `actions/python-versions` manifest at revision 0bd7e714 lists 3.10.22 for Ubuntu 24.04 x64, and the `test (3.10)` job of `ci` run 42529224187 on 2026-11-08 passed), and raising the floor would refuse an install on 3.10 with no change in the code. It leaves the matrix, with `requires-python` raised to `>=3.11` in the same commit, when setup-python can no longer install it on the pinned runner or the code needs a 3.11 feature. 3.15 is not in the matrix yet, although the same manifest lists 3.15.2 for Ubuntu 24.04 x64: the pre-push check runs the suite locally on every matrix version, and on 2026-11-08 the interpreter installer it uses offered only the pre-release 3.15.0a8, so a 3.15 job would be one that check could not repeat; 3.15 joins the matrix once a final 3.15 runs there. Tests in `tests/test_ci_pythons.py` (standard library only) require the matrix to be the list this bullet writes, with quoted `X.Y` items, consecutive and ascending, the matrix block to hold that list alone and no other `python-version` line but reads of `matrix.python-version` (v1.120), the `requires-python` floor to be its first version and the README line, in `## Development`, to name its first and last, and each of 14 broken variants (10 to v1.119) of those four files fails the check
- Steps, in this order: the checkout, the Python, `Install` (`python -m pip install -e .`, the install line every one of those README regenerate blocks starts with), `Regenerate` (one `run` block whose lines are, in order, the README regenerate commands of `### AutoBench`, `### AutoBench judges`, `### tau2-bench` (v1.55), `### LiveCodeBench` (v1.65), `### LLMRouterBench` (v1.168), `### LLM Routing Response Bank` (v1.299), `### Delegation on AutoBench` and `### Delegation on tau2-bench` (v1.80) and `### Delegation on LLMRouterBench` (v1.200), in README heading order, each block without its install line and, from v1.215, without a line the repeated-line bullet below leaves out; the `--table` lines are kept so the log shows the regenerated tables; the converters download the pinned files and check their sizes and sha256 as in 12.9 and 12.14, the tau2-bench one 12 run files of 295,933,742 bytes in all from `raw.githubusercontent.com`, and, v1.65, the LiveCodeBench one its 67 files of 1,233,548,121 bytes in all from there, each path URL-quoted as in 12.16, and, v1.168, the LLMRouterBench one its one file of 1,283,503,080 bytes from `huggingface.co` (12.18), and, v1.299, the response-bank one its four files of 1,018,191,580 bytes in all from `huggingface.co` (12.20); v1.80: the tau2-bench delegation block's converter line, `--calls`, finds the 12 run files already in the cache the `### tau2-bench` line filled, and `fetch` checks each one's size and sha256 again before it reads them; v1.84: the `### Delegation on tau2-bench` lines are both of its blocks in order, the per-domain one and then the pooled one, which has no install line, and the pooled converter line, `--calls --task-by all`, reads the same cached files), and `Compare` (`git diff --exit-code --` followed by every `--out` path under `evidence/` of those commands, in command order: from v1.65 `evidence/autobench-2026/results.json`, `evidence/autobench-judges/results.json`, `evidence/tau2-bench/results.json`, `evidence/livecodebench/results.json`, `evidence/delegation/results.json` and `evidence/delegation/variants.json`, from v1.80 `evidence/tau2-bench-delegation/results.json` after them, from v1.84 `evidence/tau2-bench-delegation/pooled.json` last, from v1.168 `evidence/llmrouterbench/results.json` directly after `evidence/livecodebench/results.json`, from v1.200 `evidence/llmrouterbench-delegation/results.json` last and from v1.299 `evidence/response-bank/results.json` directly after `evidence/llmrouterbench/results.json` (v1.55 to v1.64 the same without the LiveCodeBench file; to v1.54 the three AutoBench-based `results.json` files and, from v1.48, `variants.json` after them, since the delegation README block then ends with the two Variant points lines of 13.10)), so the run fails when a regenerated file differs by one byte from the committed one. Each step holds only its keys: `uses` and `with` for the two actions, `name` and `run` for the three named steps, so no step has an `if`, `continue-on-error`, `shell`, `working-directory` or `env`

- One run of a repeated line (v1.215, row CI8): `Regenerate` leaves out a README line identical to one it has already run when (1) the line names at least one `.json` or `.jsonl` file, (2) it names each of them once and appends to none (`>>`), and (3) no line run since the last run of that line names a file of the same base name other than as the argument directly after `--outcomes`, `--matrix`, `--evaluate` or `--table`, the flags that only read a file; so an `--out` path, an `--out=` form, a `>` target or any other mention (a `--registry` path, which the AutoBench converters write) between the two keeps the line, as does a line that names no such file, names one twice (it may read what it writes) or appends. Here a file is a run of characters, none of them whitespace, `=`, `<`, `>`, `|`, `;`, `&`, `'` or `"`, that ends in `.json` or `.jsonl` and has no letter, digit, `_` or `.` directly after it, and two files are the same when their base names (the part after the last `/`) are: so `--out=x.jsonl` and `>x.jsonl` each name `x.jsonl`, `tmp/x.jsonl` counts as `x.jsonl`, `x.json.bak` names no file, and a read flag in the `--outcomes=x.jsonl` form is not one argument after the flag, so it counts as a mention. The reason: such a line would write the same bytes to files that no line has changed since it wrote them, from the same input, since a converter's other input is its pinned download, which it checks by size and sha256 and which no line of the step writes; what leaving it out changes is the log, which loses the line's second report on standard output (for the converter, its JSON summary, the same as the first), and no file `Compare` reads. The README blocks stay whole, each subsection's commands as a reader runs them alone, so the rule applies only to the joined lines of the workflow. Today it leaves out one line, the converter line of `### Delegation on LLMRouterBench`, `python evidence/llmrouterbench/convert.py --out llmrouterbench-matrix.jsonl`, byte for byte that of `### LLMRouterBench`; between the two only the `### LLMRouterBench` evaluate line names `llmrouterbench-matrix.jsonl`, after `--outcomes`; so `Regenerate` runs 36 of the 37 README commands (from v1.299 40 of the 41, since the response-bank lines between the two copies name none of its files). In run 42814244636 on `4ec6dfa` (2026-11-10), the first with the v1.200 lines, 271 seconds from its start to its last update, that second run took about 39 seconds by the log's timestamps, from 12:18:37.86 UTC (the last line the pooled tau2-bench table printed) to 12:19:17.25 (the first line the converter printed). The time limit kept its value and basis here; v1.217 restates it from the first run with this change, v1.225 from the slowest of the first four, v1.268 from the slowest of the first eleven, v1.301 from the first run on the v1.299 lines, v1.302 from the slower of the first two and v1.321 from the slower of the first two runs on the v1.317 lines (the time-limit bullet). The column-0 comment at the top of the workflow gives the rule, after the RouterBench reason, in the sentence `A README line identical to one already run in the step is not run again when no line between them names one of its files except to read it (DESIGN.md 12.13).` Tests in `tests/test_evidence_workflow.py` build the expected `Regenerate` lines from the README commands by this rule; check it on 10 hand-built lists (a file only read between the two copies leaves the repeat out; one written by `--out`, `--out=` or `>`, named after `--registry`, or named in another folder with the same base name keeps it, as do a line naming no file, an in-place line and an appending one; a third copy is compared with the last copy run, not the first), 12 from v1.216 (an `--outcomes=` mention between keeps the repeat, and a line naming only `x.json.bak` is kept); require the README commands to give exactly that one line to leave out; and require each of 2 broken workflows to fail the one check it names: the repeated converter line kept before the 13.13 script line (the `Regenerate` check) and the sentence dropped from the top comment (the comment check)
- README: the evidence section's opening paragraph (12.8) ends with the sentence that names the workflow, links the file, names the subsections it reruns (from v1.168 `the AutoBench, AutoBench judges, tau2-bench, LiveCodeBench, LLMRouterBench and delegation subsections`, from v1.299 `the AutoBench, AutoBench judges, tau2-bench, LiveCodeBench, LLMRouterBench, LLM Routing Response Bank and delegation subsections`) and the results files of the `Compare` line (three to v1.47, four from v1.48, five from v1.55, six from v1.65, seven from v1.80, eight from v1.84, nine from v1.168, ten from v1.200, eleven from v1.299) and gives the RouterBench reason; the test requires it verbatim. From v1.299 the `### LLM Routing Response Bank` regenerate line says, as the `### LLMRouterBench` one does, that the `evidence` workflow reruns these commands on GitHub and fails if `results.json` changes by a byte, where to v1.298 it said the workflow does not rerun them yet (12.8)
- Tests, in `tests/test_evidence_workflow.py` (standard library only, so they run in CI): the workflow is read with a line parser for this file's shape, and the test requires the triggers, permissions, default shell, the job header, the actions, the Python version, the step names, order and keys, the install line, the `Regenerate` lines rebuilt from the README blocks (from v1.215 by the repeated-line rule above; one `bash` block per rerun subsection, and from v1.84 two in `### Delegation on tau2-bench`, read in order; a subsection with another count fails) and the `Compare` line rebuilt from their `--out` paths, and the top comment's RouterBench reason, from v1.278 its list of the rerun subsections and the width of every line above `on:` (the RouterBench bullet above), with a test that the README holds those eight (nine from v1.299) `### ` headings in that order; a comment or blank line inside a block is read as part of it; it fails on a secret, a `pull_request_target` trigger or any `write` permission, by substring, so a comment that names them fails too; and each of 16 broken workflows (17 from v1.84, which adds a `Compare` line without `pooled.json`, 22 from v1.152, with the five of the time-limit bullet, and 23 from v1.168, which adds a `Compare` line without `evidence/llmrouterbench/results.json`, and 24 from v1.200, which adds one without `evidence/llmrouterbench-delegation/results.json`, and 26 from v1.215, which adds the two of the repeated-line bullet, and 28 from v1.251, which adds the two of the no-cache bullet, and 30 from v1.278, which adds `LiveCodeBench` dropped from the top comment's list and the comment's first two lines, lines 3 and 4 of the file, joined into one, which leaves the joined comment as it was, each failing the one check it names, and 31 from v1.299, which adds a `Compare` line without `evidence/response-bank/results.json`) fails the check (from v1.85 the two `Compare` mutants, without `variants.json` and without `pooled.json`, on the `Compare` check alone, from v1.168 the three, from v1.200 the four and from v1.299 the five), among them a README command dropped, swapped or added, a changed `paths` list, a write permission, a job shell without `pipefail`, an `if: false` on `Regenerate`, a `continue-on-error` on `Compare`, a `working-directory`, a comment inside `Regenerate` and a second job; from v1.49 the runner check requires in each of `ci.yml` and the workflow one `runs-on` naming a versioned `ubuntu-XX.YY` image, the same in both, and the comment above `jobs:` naming it with a date that `datetime.date.fromisoformat` reads, and each of 6 broken pairs fails it: either file back on `ubuntu-latest`, the two files on different images, the `ci.yml` comment dropped, the workflow's comment without its date, and the `ci.yml` comment with a date that does not exist. The run itself is checked on GitHub, not by the suite

### 12.14 Public evidence: the tau2-bench converter (`evidence/tau2-bench/convert.py`, v1.50; the `--out` check v1.171, on seven CLIs and `--registry` v1.174)
Purpose: a third public outcome matrix in the 12.1 format, on an agentic task whose label comes from program checks and whose cost was recorded per conversation, the dataset `research/datasets.md` recommends. Standard library only (argparse, hashlib, json, math, re, sys, urllib, pathlib), so it needs no extra; the package never imports it
- Source, pinned: the GitHub repo `sierra-research/tau2-bench` at commit `REVISION = "033bf082f48e4a3c6a1256208e93cf2ce9c3bb6f"`, folder `data/tau2/results/final`, `LICENCE = "MIT"` (the repo's LICENSE file), `BASE_URL = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sierra-research/tau2-bench/<REVISION>/data/tau2/results/final/"`. `FILES` maps each of the 12 default run files to its byte size and sha256, in ascending name order: the agents `claude-3-7-sonnet-20250219`, `gpt-4.1-2025-04-14`, `gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14` and `o4-mini-2025-04-16`, each in the domains `airline`, `retail` and `telecom`, named `<agent>_<domain>_<mode>_gpt-4.1-2025-04-14_4trials.json` with `<mode>` `base` for `gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14` and `default` for the others; 295,933,742 bytes in all, 7,599,444 to 51,038,687 each. The folder's other 14 run files (the telecom `op`, `no-user` and `no-user-op` ablations and the `telecom-workflow` domain, for two of the agents) are not read. The folder has no checksum file, so the pins in the script are the only trust root
- `default_cache_dir()` is `~/.cache/ai-model-routing-evidence/tau2-bench/` (from `Path.home()`), never inside the repo. `fetch(cache_dir=None) -> Path` creates the folder if needed and returns it after, for each file in `FILES` order: a present file is verified and kept, and one that fails `verify` is a `ValueError` naming it and is left in place; a missing one is downloaded with `urllib.request.urlopen` from `BASE_URL` plus its name into `<name>.part`, verified, then renamed, and the `.part` file is removed when the download or the check fails. Each download has a timeout of 60 seconds, and any error but a `ValueError` while downloading is raised as an `OSError` that names the file (an HTTP protocol error, such as an incomplete chunked read, is not an `OSError` and would otherwise escape the CLI's one-line error) (v1.51). `verify(path, size, sha256)` as in 12.6
- `model_id(llm) -> str`, a rule over the run's `llm` name, not a list: a name containing `/` is a `ValueError` (a routing or platform prefix is never guessed away); `claude-<rest>` becomes `anthropic/claude-<rest>`; `gpt-<rest>`, and a name that starts with `o` and a digit (`o4-mini-2025-04-16`), become `openai/<name>`; `<rest>` must be non-empty, and any other name, an empty string or a non-string is a `ValueError` rather than a guess
- `extract(run, trial=0, tokens=False) -> dict` reduces one parsed run file to what the matrix needs, so the converter holds one run file in memory at a time:
  - `ValueError`: first the arguments, a `trial` that is a bool, not an int or `< 0`, and a `tokens` that is not a bool; then a `run` that is not a dict or has no `info`, `tasks` or `simulations`; an `info` that is not a dict; an `info.environment_info.domain_name`, `info.agent_info.llm` or `info.user_info.llm` that is not a non-empty string (the message names the key path); a domain containing `|`; an agent or user name that `model_id` rejects, the agent first (these three in that order, so a missing user name is reported before a domain containing `|`, v1.51); `tasks` not a list, a task whose `id` is not a non-empty string, or a repeated task id; `simulations` not a list. Then each simulation in file order, so the first bad simulation decides the error: one that is not a dict, whose `task_id` is not a task id of the file, whose `trial` is a bool, not an int or `< 0`, or whose (`task_id`, `trial`) repeats an earlier one; and, only when its trial is the chosen one, a `reward_info.reward` that is a bool, not an int or float, or not exactly 0 or 1 (a partial reward is refused, never rounded), an `agent_cost` that is a bool, not an int or float, not finite (an int too large for a float counts as not finite, v1.51) or `< 0`, and with `tokens`, `messages` that is not a list, a message that is not a dict, or an agent message (role `assistant`) with no `usage` key (each of the 73,540 agent messages in the pinned files has one, 4,448 of them `null`, v1.51) or whose `usage` is neither `null` nor a dict whose `prompt_tokens` and `completion_tokens` are ints `>= 0` that are not bools. Last, a file in which no simulation has the chosen trial. A message about a simulation names the domain, the agent ID and the task id, and a reward or cost message also names its key (`reward`, `agent_cost`)
  - returns the keys `domain` (the `domain_name` as written), `agent` and `user` (each as `model_id` maps it), `trial`, `tokens`, `cells` and `missing_trial`, in that order. `cells` maps each task id that has a simulation at the chosen trial, ascending, to `{"correct": reward == 1, "cost_usd": float(agent_cost)}`, a negative zero written as `0.0`, and with `tokens` also `input_tokens` and `output_tokens`, the sums of `prompt_tokens` and `completion_tokens` over the agent messages whose `usage` is not `null`. `missing_trial` lists, ascending, the file's task ids with no simulation at that trial. User and tool messages are never counted, and with `tokens` False no message is read
- `convert(extracts, task_by="domain") -> tuple[list[dict], dict]` over a list of `extract` results:
  - `ValueError`, checked before any output: a `task_by` other than `domain` and `all`; `extracts` that is not a non-empty list or tuple; extracts that differ in `user`, `trial` or `tokens` (a matrix holds the user simulator and the trial fixed); two extracts for one (domain, agent); a domain with no extract for an agent that appears in another domain
  - an item is a task id of a domain, written `<domain>|<task id>` under either `task_by`, so `all` pools the same item IDs and 12.2 splits them the same way; it is kept only when every agent has a cell for it, and a task id that some agent lacks (any task id of that domain in a `cells` or `missing_trial`) is dropped and counted, never scored as a miss. The task is the domain, or `all` for every item with `task_by` `all`
  - one row per kept item and agent with the keys `task`, `item`, `model`, `correct`, `cost_usd` in that order, then `input_tokens` and `output_tokens` with `tokens`; rows sorted by (task, item, model) as strings; they form the complete matrix 12.3 requires, and the result does not depend on the order of the extracts or of the tasks and simulations in a run; the input is not modified
  - the report has the keys `models` (agent IDs ascending), `user_models` (a one-entry list), `trial`, `task_by`, `kept` and `dropped_incomplete` (each `{task: items}`, tasks ascending, every task in both, zeros included) and `rows` (the number of rows)
- `read_run(path)` reads one UTF-8 JSON file, and a file that is not valid UTF-8 or not valid JSON, or that nests deeper than the parser's recursion limit, is a `ValueError` naming it (v1.51); `write_jsonl(rows, path)` as in 12.6
- CLI: `python evidence/tau2-bench/convert.py --out PATH [--cache DIR] [--trial N] [--task-by {domain,all}] [--tokens]`, entry point `main(argv=None) -> int`. Options are checked first, so a bad one downloads nothing: `--trial` is decimal digits only (default 0), `--task-by` one of the two (default `domain`), and `--out` must pass the `--out` check, which 12.6, 12.9, 12.11, 12.16, 12.18 and 13.11 use too (v1.171, row H4; 12.6, 12.9 and 12.11 from v1.174, row H4b; to v1.170 this CLI refused only an empty path, `.` and `/`, v1.52 review): the last `/`-separated part of the value is not empty, `.` or `..`, so `..`, `x/..`, `x/.` and a value ending in `/` are bad; neither the path nor `<out>.part` is an existing folder, a link to one included; and the path's parent is an existing folder. A value that fails is a bad option with one `error: --out ...` line, refused before the fetch, so it downloads and writes nothing; to v1.170 each of the four CLIs let some of these through (all four an existing folder, `x/.` and a missing parent folder), so the download ran and only the write or the final rename failed. The check names the option it reads (v1.174): 12.9 and 12.11 run it on `--registry` too when it is given, after `--out` and before their four-files check, so a bad `--registry` is one `error: --registry ...` line and downloads nothing; to v1.173 the RouterBench converter (12.6) and both AutoBench CLIs (12.9, 12.11) checked neither option before the fetch, so each of the twelve bad values below downloaded first. The three results scripts (12.7, 13.10, 13.12) do not take the check: they download nothing and write through `<out>.part` renamed onto `--out`, so a bad `--out` fails at their write or rename after local reads only, with their own one `error:` line. `tests/test_evidence_out_option.py` runs the check on the seven CLIs (four to v1.173; for `judges.py` the stand-in replaces the `fetch` of the `convert` module it loads): twelve bad values (an empty path, `.`, `/`, `..`, `sub/..`, `sub/.`, `m.jsonl/`, an existing folder by its absolute path, a link to one, `m.jsonl` beside a folder `m.jsonl.part`, `nodir/m.jsonl`, and from v1.173 `afile/m.jsonl` where `afile` is a regular file), each with `fetch` replaced by one that records its call and raises, give 2, one `error: --out` line, nothing on stdout, no new file and no `fetch` call; `m.jsonl`, `sub/m.jsonl`, an absolute path in `sub` and, from v1.173, an existing regular file, which is left as it was, get past the check to `fetch`; and the CLIs' `_check_out` sources are one text (v1.173; from v1.174 six copies and, for `judges.py`, which defines none of its own, the one of the `convert` module it loads). From v1.174 the twelve bad values given as `--registry` beside `--out out.jsonl` to 12.9 and 12.11 give 2, one `error: --registry` line, nothing on stdout, no new file and no `fetch` call, the four good values as `--registry` get past the check to `fetch`, and with both options bad the one line names `--out`, while `--out m.jsonl --registry m.jsonl/` names `--registry`, not the four-files error. Then it fetches, reads and extracts the files one at a time in `FILES` order, converts, writes `<out>.part`, prints the report as `json.dumps(report, indent=2)`, flushing stdout, and only then renames it onto `--out` (v1.94, as 13.11), so a write to stdout that fails (a closed pipe) is an I/O error that leaves `--out` as it was. After a `BrokenPipeError` from the print, stdout is pointed at `os.devnull` as in 13.11 (v1.150), so the process exits 2 with that one `error:` line, not 120 with two more lines from the interpreter's flush at exit. It returns 0 on success and 2 with exactly one `error: ...` line on stderr (a carriage return or line feed in the message written as `\r` or `\n`) and nothing on stdout for a missing or bad option, a size or hash mismatch, invalid data or an I/O error, but for a rename that fails after the print, the one failure with the report already on stdout; on any failure `--out` is not written (an existing one is left as it was) and `<out>.part` is removed
- Choices (v1.50):
  - one trial per task: each run holds 4 trials of every task and 12.1 takes one row per (task, item, model). With each trial as its own item, the 12.2 split, which hashes the item ID, would put trials of one task in both halves, so the held-out half would score tasks the calibration half has seen. So one trial is kept, trial 0 by default and the others as a stated sensitivity. In all 12 pinned files trial t ran with the same seed for every agent (626729, 373753, 361454 and 1567 for trials 0 to 3), so a row compares the agents under one simulated-user seed, though the user model still answers each agent's own messages
  - cost: `cost_usd` is the run's recorded `agent_cost`, the agent model's calls in that conversation. On the pinned files it equals the `math.fsum` of the agent messages' `cost` values within 4.5e-16, and the files do not name the price table behind it. None of the 4 agents is in `models.json`, so pricing the token counts would need a dated price list the repo does not hold; `--tokens` adds the summed counts for a later delegation run or a cross-check. `user_cost`, the user simulator's calls, is never counted: it is the benchmark's cost, not the routed call's
  - label: only the number `reward_info.reward` is read, the result of the run's program checks (research/datasets.md lists the reward bases). 36 of the 4,448 simulations in the 12 files ended at `max_steps` rather than `user_stop`, and their reward is used as recorded
  - tasks: one routing task per domain by default. Airline has 50 tasks, and the 12.2 split draws each item's half from its hash, so the airline calibration half holds 16 to 35 items over seeds 0 to 49 and fewer than the default `min_samples` of 30 (5.3) on 40 of those 50 seeds (7 of seeds 0 to 9); on such a seed no model has enough calibration items to clear any bar there at that default, while on the other 10 a model can clear a low enough bar. Retail and telecom hold 45 to 65 and 44 to 68. `--task-by all` pools the 278 items into one task (114 to 158). The results slice states which matrix it commits, with its split sizes and bars
  - regeneration: when this was written the 12 files downloaded in about 6 seconds and converted in about 1 second, with no extra, so the results slice adds the converter to the 12.13 workflow rather than leaving it local like RouterBench
- Default options on the pinned files (checked with a private prototype of this contract when this section was written; the cached-file test pins them): 4 models, the user model `openai/gpt-4.1-2025-04-14`, kept airline 50, retail 114 and telecom 114 items, none dropped, 1,112 rows, and the same counts at every trial from 0 to 3. At trial 0 the agents' success rates per domain span 0.5 to 0.58 (airline), 0.684 to 0.798 (retail) and 0.368 to 0.439 (telecom); `cost_usd` spans 0.00136 to 6.446604; with `--tokens`, `input_tokens` spans 6,629 to 2,096,093 and `output_tokens` 123 to 26,531. Over all 4 trials the `agent_cost` values sum to 487.623954 for `claude-3-7-sonnet-20250219` and 29.755562 for `gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14`, the "Sum cost per LLM" values of the folder's `figs/cost_info.txt` (whose sums for the other two agents also count their ablation runs)
- v1.76, per-call rows for the delegation harness (13.9 v1.74): a mode that writes each conversation's work calls, so a tau2-bench item can be priced on its own calls:
  - `extract(run, trial=0, tokens=False, calls=False)`: `calls` is checked after `tokens`, a `ValueError` when it is not a bool (`calls ... is not a bool`), and `calls` True with `tokens` False is a `ValueError` whose message starts `calls needs tokens`, since a per-call row carries the summed counts too. Every extract gains the key `calls` after `tokens` (the v1.50 key list moves once). With `calls`, each cell gains `call_input_tokens` and `call_output_tokens` after `output_tokens`: over the agent messages whose `usage` is not `null`, in message order, with p_j and c_j the `prompt_tokens` and `completion_tokens` of the j-th of them and K their number, `call_output_tokens` is c_1 to c_K and `call_input_tokens` is p_1, then p_j - p_{j-1} - c_{j-1} for j from 2 to K, the 13.9 a_j if every prompt re-sends the whole history. An entry of `call_input_tokens` may be negative: the extract records what the usage says, and `convert` applies the 13.9 rule; `call_output_tokens` entries are the completion counts, which the v1.50 usage check already holds to ints `>= 0`, so only a new input can be negative. Because p_j = a_1 + ... + a_j + c_1 + ... + c_{j-1} whatever the signs, `input_tokens` = p_1 + ... + p_K is the 13.9 Rd of the two lists and `output_tokens` is their O. A simulation at the chosen trial with no such message is a `ValueError` whose message, like the other simulation messages, names the domain, the agent ID and the task id (as a quoted repr) and contains `has no model call` (13.9 needs non-empty lists); the pinned files have none at any trial from 0 to 3. Without `calls` the cells, and with `tokens` False the messages, are read as before
  - `convert`: the check that the extracts agree adds `calls` after `tokens`, with the label `calls setting`. With `calls`, after the v1.50 checks, an agent any of whose cells, in any of its extracts, has a negative `call_input_tokens` entry is left out of the matrix with all its rows, the 13.9 rule for completion counts that include reasoning a later call does not re-read; `models` lists the other agents, and an item is kept when each of them has a cell for it, so the kept and dropped counts read those agents only; when every agent is left out, a `ValueError` whose message starts `every agent has a call whose new input is negative`. The rows of the kept agents gain `call_input_tokens` and `call_output_tokens` after `output_tokens`, as copies of the cell's lists (new list objects, so changing a row never changes an extract), and the report gains `left_out` as its last key, `{agent: the number of negative entries over its cells}` with agents ascending, `{}` when none is left out. Without `calls`, the rows and the report are the v1.50 ones bit for bit, with no `left_out` key
  - CLI: `--calls` adds the per-call lists and implies `--tokens`, and otherwise `main` behaves as above: it returns 0 and prints the report, now with `left_out`, on success, and 2 with one `error:` line on a failure; the usage line becomes `python evidence/tau2-bench/convert.py --out PATH [--cache DIR] [--trial N] [--task-by {domain,all}] [--tokens] [--calls]`. Every 13.9 row check then passes on the written rows, and `evaluate_delegation` reads them with `expected_work_calls` on each cell
  - On the pinned files at trial 0 (checked with a private prototype of this contract when this was written; the cached-file test pins them): `--calls` leaves out `openai/o4-mini-2025-04-16`, with 2,962 negative entries over its 3,849 calls after a first one (`research/delegation-datasets.md`); keeps `anthropic/claude-3-7-sonnet-20250219`, `openai/gpt-4.1-2025-04-14` and `openai/gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14` on the 50 airline, 114 retail and 114 telecom items, none dropped, 834 rows, whose `input_tokens` and `output_tokens` equal those of the `--tokens` rows. K runs from 2 to 100 calls (median 14): 5 to 100 (median 15) for the Anthropic agent, 2 to 99 (12) and 3 to 100 (14) for the two GPT-4.1 agents; the first call's read from 3,252 to 8,245 tokens; the largest last call reads 33,765 tokens. At trials 1 and 2 `openai/gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14` also has 2 and 1 negative entries, so `--calls` keeps 2 agents and 556 rows there, and at trial 3 it keeps 3 agents and 834 rows (o4-mini 2,880), so a trial sensitivity of the delegation results compares 3 agents at trials 0 and 3 and 2 at trials 1 and 2. The written file is 341,633 bytes, so, like the 12.14 matrix, it is never committed: a results slice commits what the harness computes from it
